What brings you in today?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / maid_or_housekeeping_cleaner / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I'm here because people keep acting like I'm imagining it, but I know the hospital people and some folks from the agency have been watching our house and my cleaning routes to see if I'm taking proper care of my husband.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… I’m here because people keep telling me I’m imagining things, or that I’m “reading into it,” but I know what I’ve seen. The hospital people, and some people from the agency I work through, they’ve been watching our house and even the cleaning routes I take. It’s like they’re checking up on me, seeing if I’m taking proper care of my husband, like they don’t trust me to do it right.

And it’s not just a feeling, you know? It’s little things—the way people act, the timing of things, how they seem to know where I’ve been. I clean houses, so I’m out on different routes, and I notice when someone is paying attention. I’ve done this work a long time. I’m not stupid. But when I try to explain it, people look at me like I’m making it up, and that’s why I came in, because I need somebody to actually listen instead of just brushing me off.

No… no, it isn’t only when I’m wound up like that. I know why you’re asking, because I do have those stretches where I’m up early and doing too much, but there was a quieter time too—several weeks, I’d say—where I wasn’t rushing around the same way.

I was sleeping more then, and I wasn’t taking on so much or moving so fast. My family probably would’ve said I was calmer, or at least not constantly trying to get everything done at once. But even during that time, I still heard that woman’s voice by the kitchen window. And I still had that feeling—well, more than a feeling to me—that the agency people were watching us, checking on the house and whether I was handling things right.

So no, it doesn’t just happen when I have all that energy. It can happen even when I’m slowed down.

And I still had that feeling—well, more than a feeling to me—that the agency people were watching us, checking on the house and whether I was handling things right.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t just happen when I have all that energy. Yes… it’s definitely been longer than a month. I wish I could say it was just a short spell, like a bad week or something, but it’s been going on for quite a while now.

mood episode\n  manifestation: There was a quieter spell for several weeks when I was sleeping more and not rushing around as much, but I still heard the voice by the kitchen window and believed the agency was watching us.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My cleaning agency took away my supervisory routes because clients and coworkers said they could not rely on my work the way they used to. At home, my husband and pastor have had to step in more because I am not keeping up with caregiving and household responsibilities safely.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: This whole change has been going on for about 1 year and 2 months, since after my husband's cardiac hospitalization.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 11 months\n  manifestation: The voices and the feeling that people are watching us have been happening for about 11 months now.\n- key: manic_episode_duration\n  name: Manic Episode Duration\n  description: 10 months\n  manifestation: The stretches of being unusually energized, irritable, and barely sleeping have been happening on and off for about 10 months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / maid_or_housekeeping_cleaner / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S019\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you had a time when you felt unusually high or energized for days at a time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have times like that. It’s not like I’m trying to be difficult or anything, but I’ll get up before it’s even daylight, before most people are thinking about moving, and I’m already cleaning, getting things straightened, starting food, checking what needs to be done.\n\nAnd then one thing turns into another. My family probably would’ve said I was calmer, or at least not constantly trying to get everything done at once. But even during that time, I still heard that woman’s voice by the kitchen window. And I still had that feeling—well, more than a feeling to me—that the agency people were watching us, checking on the house and whether I was handling things right.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t just happen when I have all that energy. Not every single minute of every day, but it keeps coming back, and it’s been part of my life for most of this past year. Yes… yes, I have. I don’t like saying that, because I’ve always been someone who worked hard, especially with cleaning. I knew my routes, I knew what clients liked, and I used to be the person they trusted to keep things moving.

even when everyone tells me to slow down.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).\n  manifestation: I started feeling like I was the only one with the sense and strength to keep my husband, my home, and even some of the church people safe from what was coming.\n- key: S036\n  name: Decreased_Sleep\n  description: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).\n  manifestation: Many nights I sleep two or three hours and wake up ready to I wish I could say it was just a short spell, like a bad week or something, but it’s been going on for quite a while now.\n\nIf I think back on it, the voice and that feeling like people are watching us or checking on us… it’s been about eleven months. Not every single minute of every day, but it keeps coming back, and it’s been part of my life for most of this past year. It’s hard to admit that out loud, because when you say “eleven months” it sounds so much bigger than when you’re just trying to get through each day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any brain or nervous system illness?\n\nPatient: No… not that I know of. I mean, nobody has ever sat me down and told me I have some brain illness or nerve disease or anything like that.\n\nI’ve had regular aches and tiredness like people get, and I’ve been to doctors for ordinary things over the years, but as far as something with my brain or nervous system… I don’t have a diagnosis like that. If there was something in my chart, nobody explained it to me that way.\n\nDoctor: Since these problems began, have you been less able to do your work or take care of things at home than you were before?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I don’t like saying that, because I’ve always been someone who worked hard, especially with cleaning. I knew my routes, I knew what clients liked, and I used to be the person they trusted to keep things moving.\n\nBut since all this started, the agency took away my supervisory routes. They didn’t just say it out of nowhere—there were complaints, I guess, from clients and coworkers saying they couldn’t rely on my work the way they used to. That hurt a lot. My pastor too, actually. I used to manage so much of that myself, and now it feels like other people are having to watch over the things I should be handling. I’m not really sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… I’ve had times where I’ve felt really worn down and upset, and some days I just don’t feel like myself, but if you’re asking whether there was a clear two-week stretch where I was depressed most of the day nearly every day… I don’t know that I can say that for sure.

I don’t want to say yes if I’m not remembering it right. Sometimes everything kind of blends together when you’re just trying to keep going day by day. There have been bad days, definitely, but I’m not sure I noticed it in that kind of steady, every-day-for-two-weeks way.

It’s like they’re checking up on me, seeing if I’m taking proper care of my husband, like they don’t trust me to do it right.\n\nAnd it’s not just a feeling, you know? I’ve done this work a long time. I’m not stupid. But when I try to explain it, people look at me like I’m making it up, and that’s why I came in, because I need somebody to actually listen instead of just brushing me off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I do hear a voice sometimes, but it’s not like how people make it sound. It’s not constant, and I’m not just sitting there making it up. It usually happens when the house is quiet, like when everything has settled down and I’m near the kitchen. I’ll hear a woman’s voice by the kitchen window, very clear, like she’s standing right there outside or just close enough that I should be able to see her.\n\nShe’ll say things like I need to check the locks, or that I should move my husband’s pills. And I know how that sounds, but it doesn’t feel like a thought in my own head. It sounds like someone speaking to me, plain as anything. I’ll look, and no one is there, but I still heard it. So yes, I guess the answer is yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a time when you felt unusually high or energized for days at a time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have times like that. It’s not like I’m trying to be difficult or anything, but I’ll get up before it’s even daylight, before most people are thinking about moving, and I’m already cleaning, getting things straightened, starting food, checking what needs to be done.\n\nAnd then one thing turns into another. I’ll be making lists, then making another list because the first one wasn’t organized right, then I’m calling about appointments or trying to set things up, and before you know it I’ve taken on church errands too—like someone needs something dropped off, or there’s a call to make, or something to organize, and I’ll say, “I can do it,” even if my family is telling me I need to sit down and slow myself.\n\nBut at the time it doesn’t feel wrong to me. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think there was. I’m trying to place it right, because I don’t want to make it sound more exact than it was, but there was a quieter spell for several weeks where I wasn’t all keyed up like before. I was sleeping more then, and I wasn’t rushing around so much or acting like I had to get everything done right that second.

But even during that calmer time, I still heard the voice by the kitchen window. And I still had that feeling that the agency was watching us. It didn’t go away just because I wasn’t as energized. I also don’t think I was deeply depressed then, not like lying there crying all day or anything like that. It was more like things had slowed down, but the watching feeling and the voice were still there in the background… sometimes not even background, honestly. It still felt real to me.

I’ll be making lists, then making another list because the first one wasn’t organized right, then I’m calling about appointments or trying to set things up, and before you know it I’ve taken on church errands too—like someone needs something dropped off, or there’s a call to make, or something to organize, and I’ll say, “I can do it,” even if my family is telling me I need to sit down and slow myself.\n\nBut at the time it doesn’t feel wrong to me. I wish I could say it was just a short spell, like a bad week or something, but it’s been going on for quite a while now.\n\nIf I think back on it, the voice and that feeling like people are watching us or checking on us… it’s been about eleven months. Not every single minute of every day, but it keeps coming back, and it’s been part of my life for most of this past year. It’s hard to admit that out loud, because when you say “eleven months” it sounds so much bigger than when you’re just trying to get through each day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any brain or nervous system illness?\n\nPatient: No… not that I know of. I mean, nobody has ever sat me down and told me I have some brain illness or nerve disease or anything like that.\n\nI’ve had regular aches and tiredness like people get, and I’ve been to doctors for ordinary things over the years, but as far as something with my brain or nervous system… I don’t have a diagnosis like that. If there was something in my chart, nobody explained it to me that way.\n\nDoctor: Since these problems began, have you been less able to do your work or take care of things at home than you were before?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I don’t like saying that, because I’ve always been someone who worked hard, especially with cleaning. I knew my routes, I knew what clients liked, and I used to be the person they trusted to keep things moving.\n\nBut since all this started, the agency took away my supervisory routes. They didn’t just say it out of nowhere—there were complaints, I guess, from clients and coworkers saying they couldn’t rely on my work the way they used to. That hurt a lot. I tried to explain that I was still trying, but it’s like things were slipping, and people noticed before I wanted to admit it.\n\nAnd at home, too… my husband has had to step in more. My pastor too, actually. Not just for emotional support, but with real things—making sure the caregiving and household responsibilities are handled safely, because I haven’t been keeping up like before. I used to manage so much of that myself, and now it feels like other people are having to watch over the things I should be handling. It’s embarrassing, but yes, I’m less able than I was.\n\nDoctor: During this past year, have you had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… I’ve had times where I’ve felt really worn down and upset, and some days I just don’t feel like myself, but if you’re asking whether there was a clear two-week stretch where I was depressed most of the day nearly every day… I don’t know that I can say that for sure.\n\nI don’t want to say yes if I’m not remembering it right. Sometimes everything kind of blends together when you’re just trying to keep going day by day. There have been bad days, definitely, but I’m not sure I noticed it in that kind of steady, every-day-for-two-weeks way.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a time when the voice and the feeling of being watched lasted for at least two weeks when you were not feeling unusually energized or depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there was. I’m trying to place it right, because I don’t want to make it sound more exact than it was, but there was a quieter spell for several weeks where I wasn’t all keyed up like before. I was sleeping more then, and I wasn’t rushing around so much or acting like I had to get everything done right that second.\n\nBut even during that calmer time, I still heard the voice by the kitchen window. And I still had that feeling that the agency was watching us. It didn’t go away just because I wasn’t as energized. I also don’t think I was deeply depressed then, not like lying there crying all day or anything like that. It was more like things had slowed down, but the watching feeling and the voice were still there in the background… sometimes not even background, honestly. It still felt real to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… that definitely happened. During those times, I could sleep maybe two or three hours, and then I’d wake up like I’d had a full night. Not groggy, not dragging myself around like I normally would if I slept that little.

I’d get up and feel ready to do things right away—like scrub the floors, sort through papers, start cleaning things that didn’t even need to be cleaned at that hour. It wasn’t like when you can’t sleep and you feel miserable the next day. I felt awake, like my body didn’t need the rest. That’s part of what made it hard for me to see it as a problem at first, because I thought, well, at least I’m getting things done. But yes, I needed a lot less sleep and still felt rested.

I’m not completely sure about that one. I mean, I know I was more wound up inside during those times, and I probably had more to say just because my mind felt busy, but I don’t know if I was talking much more than usual in a way other people would have noticed.

It’s not like I’m trying to be difficult or anything, but I’ll get up before it’s even daylight, before most people are thinking about moving, and I’m already cleaning, getting things straightened, starting food, checking what needs to be done.\n\nAnd then one thing turns into another. I’ll be making lists, then making another list because the first one wasn’t organized right, then I’m calling about appointments or trying to set things up, and before you know it I’ve taken on church errands too—like someone needs something dropped off, or there’s a call to make, or something to organize, and I’ll say, “I can do it,” even if my family is telling me I need to sit down and slow myself.\n\nBut at the time it doesn’t feel wrong to me. It feels like there’s finally enough energy to get everything handled, like if I don’t do it right then and there, it’ll all fall apart or get missed. Other people will say I’m doing too much, but I feel like I’m just trying to stay on top of things.\n\nDoctor: When you hear that woman’s voice by the kitchen window, does it happen only during those times when you feel unusually energized?\n\nPatient: No… no, it isn’t only when I’m wound up like that. I know why you’re asking, because I do have those stretches where I’m up early and doing too much, but there was a quieter time too—several weeks, I’d say—where I wasn’t rushing around the same way.\n\nI was sleeping more then, and I wasn’t taking on so much or moving so fast. My family probably would’ve said I was calmer, or at least not constantly trying to get everything done at once. But even during that time, I still heard that woman’s voice by the kitchen window. And I still had that feeling—well, more than a feeling to me—that the agency people were watching us, checking on the house and whether I was handling things right.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t just happen when I have all that energy. It can happen even when I’m slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice and the sense that people are watching you been going on for longer than a month?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s definitely been longer than a month. I wish I could say it was just a short spell, like a bad week or something, but it’s been going on for quite a while now.\n\nIf I think back on it, the voice and that feeling like people are watching us or checking on us… it’s been about eleven months. Not every single minute of every day, but it keeps coming back, and it’s been part of my life for most of this past year. It’s hard to admit that out loud, because when you say “eleven months” it sounds so much bigger than when you’re just trying to get through each day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any brain or nervous system illness?\n\nPatient: No… not that I know of. I mean, nobody has ever sat me down and told me I have some brain illness or nerve disease or anything like that.\n\nI’ve had regular aches and tiredness like people get, and I’ve been to doctors for ordinary things over the years, but as far as something with my brain or nervous system… I don’t have a diagnosis like that. If there was something in my chart, nobody explained it to me that way.\n\nDoctor: Since these problems began, have you been less able to do your work or take care of things at home than you were before?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I don’t like saying that, because I’ve always been someone who worked hard, especially with cleaning. I knew my routes, I knew what clients liked, and I used to be the person they trusted to keep things moving.\n\nBut since all this started, the agency took away my supervisory routes. They didn’t just say it out of nowhere—there were complaints, I guess, from clients and coworkers saying they couldn’t rely on my work the way they used to. That hurt a lot. I tried to explain that I was still trying, but it’s like things were slipping, and people noticed before I wanted to admit it.\n\nAnd at home, too… my husband has had to step in more. My pastor too, actually. Not just for emotional support, but with real things—making sure the caregiving and household responsibilities are handled safely, because I haven’t been keeping up like before. I used to manage so much of that myself, and now it feels like other people are having to watch over the things I should be handling. It’s embarrassing, but yes, I’m less able than I was.\n\nDoctor: During this past year, have you had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… I’ve had times where I’ve felt really worn down and upset, and some days I just don’t feel like myself, but if you’re asking whether there was a clear two-week stretch where I was depressed most of the day nearly every day… I don’t know that I can say that for sure.\n\nI don’t want to say yes if I’m not remembering it right. Sometimes everything kind of blends together when you’re just trying to keep going day by day. There have been bad days, definitely, but I’m not sure I noticed it in that kind of steady, every-day-for-two-weeks way.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a time when the voice and the feeling of being watched lasted for at least two weeks when you were not feeling unusually energized or depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there was. I’m trying to place it right, because I don’t want to make it sound more exact than it was, but there was a quieter spell for several weeks where I wasn’t all keyed up like before. I was sleeping more then, and I wasn’t rushing around so much or acting like I had to get everything done right that second.\n\nBut even during that calmer time, I still heard the voice by the kitchen window. And I still had that feeling that the agency was watching us. It didn’t go away just because I wasn’t as energized. I also don’t think I was deeply depressed then, not like lying there crying all day or anything like that. It was more like things had slowed down, but the watching feeling and the voice were still there in the background… sometimes not even background, honestly. It still felt real to me.\n\nDoctor: During those high-energy periods, did you need much less sleep than usual and still feel rested?\n\nPatient: Yes… that definitely happened. During those times, I could sleep maybe two or three hours, and then I’d wake up like I’d had a full night. Not groggy, not dragging myself around like I normally would if I slept that little.\n\nI’d get up and feel ready to do things right away—like scrub the floors, sort through papers, start cleaning things that didn’t even need to be cleaned at that hour. It wasn’t like when you can’t sleep and you feel miserable the next day. I felt awake, like my body didn’t need the rest. That’s part of what made it hard for me to see it as a problem at first, because I thought, well, at least I’m getting things done. But yes, I needed a lot less sleep and still felt rested.\n\nDoctor: During those high-energy times, did you find yourself talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure about that one. I mean, I know I was more wound up inside during those times, and I probably had more to say just because my mind felt busy, but I don’t know if I was talking much more than usual in a way other people would have noticed.\n\nSometimes I’m talkative anyway if I’m upset or if I feel like I need to explain myself, and other times I keep things in. So it’s hard for me to separate what was really different from just… me trying to get my thoughts out. I don’t remember anyone specifically saying, “You’re talking too much,” or anything like that. Maybe I talked faster or went on longer than I realized, but I can’t say for sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

I’m trying to place it right, because I don’t want to make it sound more exact than it was, but there was a quieter spell for several weeks where I wasn’t all keyed up like before. I was sleeping more then, and I wasn’t rushing around so much or acting like I had to get everything done right that second.\n\nBut even during that calmer time, I still heard the voice by the kitchen window. And I still had that feeling that the agency was watching us. It didn’t go away just because I wasn’t as energized. I also don’t think I was deeply depressed then, not like lying there crying all day or anything like that. It was more like things had slowed down, but the watching feeling and the voice were still there in the background… sometimes not even background, honestly. It still felt real to me.\n\nDoctor: During those high-energy periods, did you need much less sleep than usual and still feel rested?\n\nPatient: Yes… that definitely happened. During those times, I could sleep maybe two or three hours, and then I’d wake up like I’d had a full night. Not groggy, not dragging myself around like I normally would if I slept that little.\n\nI’d get up and feel ready to do things right away—like scrub the floors, sort through papers, start cleaning things that didn’t even need to be cleaned at that hour. It wasn’t like when you can’t sleep and you feel miserable the next day. I felt awake, like my body didn’t need the rest. That’s part of what made it hard for me to see it as a problem at first, because I thought, well, at least I’m getting things done. But yes, I needed a lot less sleep and still felt rested.\n\nDoctor: During those high-energy times, did you find yourself talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure about that one. I mean, I know I was more wound up inside during those times, and I probably had more to say just because my mind felt busy, but I don’t know if I was talking much more than usual in a way other people would have noticed.\n\nSometimes I’m talkative anyway if I’m upset or if I feel like I need to explain myself, and other times I keep things in. So it’s hard for me to separate what was really different from just… me trying to get my thoughts out. I don’t remember anyone specifically saying, “You’re talking too much,” or anything like that. Maybe I talked faster or went on longer than I realized, but I can’t say for sure.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring those high-energy times, did you feel especially powerful, important, or as if you had special abilities that other people didn’t?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / maid_or_housekeeping_cleaner / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Inflated_Self_Esteem\nmanifestation: \"I started feeling like I was the only one with the sense and strength to keep my husband, my home, and even some of the church people safe from what was coming.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I did, though I don’t know if I would have called it “powerful” at the time. It felt more like I had to be the one to handle things, because nobody else was really seeing it clearly enough.

I remember feeling like I was the only one with enough sense and strength to keep my husband safe, and to keep the house safe, and even some of the people from church. Like I had a responsibility on me that other people didn’t understand. It wasn’t just, “Oh, I’m better than everyone.” It was more like… I believed I had something in me, some kind of ability to notice what was coming and stand up to it when other people couldn’t.

And I know that sounds like a lot when I say it out loud now, but at the time it felt very serious. I felt important in that way, like if I didn’t stay on top of things, something bad could happen and it would be because I failed to protect everyone.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because people keep telling me I’m imagining things, or that I’m “reading into it,” but I know what I’ve seen. The hospital people, and some people from the agency I work through, they’ve been watching our house and even the cleaning routes I take. It’s like they’re checking up on me, seeing if I’m taking proper care of my husband, like they don’t trust me to do it right.\n\nAnd it’s not just a feeling, you know? It’s little things—the way people act, the timing of things, how they seem to know where I’ve been. I clean houses, so I’m out on different routes, and I notice when someone is paying attention. I’ve done this work a long time. I’m not stupid. But when I try to explain it, people look at me like I’m making it up, and that’s why I came in, because I need somebody to actually listen instead of just brushing me off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I do hear a voice sometimes, but it’s not like how people make it sound. It’s not constant, and I’m not just sitting there making it up. It usually happens when the house is quiet, like when everything has settled down and I’m near the kitchen. I’ll hear a woman’s voice by the kitchen window, very clear, like she’s standing right there outside or just close enough that I should be able to see her.\n\nShe’ll say things like I need to check the locks, or that I should move my husband’s pills. And I know how that sounds, but it doesn’t feel like a thought in my own head. It sounds like someone speaking to me, plain as anything. I’ll look, and no one is there, but I still heard it. So yes, I guess the answer is yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a time when you felt unusually high or energized for days at a time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have times like that. It’s not like I’m trying to be difficult or anything, but I’ll get up before it’s even daylight, before most people are thinking about moving, and I’m already cleaning, getting things straightened, starting food, checking what needs to be done.\n\nAnd then one thing turns into another. I’ll be making lists, then making another list because the first one wasn’t organized right, then I’m calling about appointments or trying to set things up, and before you know it I’ve taken on church errands too—like someone needs something dropped off, or there’s a call to make, or something to organize, and I’ll say, “I can do it,” even if my family is telling me I need to sit down and slow myself.\n\nBut at the time it doesn’t feel wrong to me. It feels like there’s finally enough energy to get everything handled, like if I don’t do it right then and there, it’ll all fall apart or get missed. Other people will say I’m doing too much, but I feel like I’m just trying to stay on top of things.\n\nDoctor: When you hear that woman’s voice by the kitchen window, does it happen only during those times when you feel unusually energized?\n\nPatient: No… no, it isn’t only when I’m wound up like that. I know why you’re asking, because I do have those stretches where I’m up early and doing too much, but there was a quieter time too—several weeks, I’d say—where I wasn’t rushing around the same way.\n\nI was sleeping more then, and I wasn’t taking on so much or moving so fast. My family probably would’ve said I was calmer, or at least not constantly trying to get everything done at once. But even during that time, I still heard that woman’s voice by the kitchen window. And I still had that feeling—well, more than a feeling to me—that the agency people were watching us, checking on the house and whether I was handling things right.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t just happen when I have all that energy. It can happen even when I’m slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice and the sense that people are watching you been going on for longer than a month?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s definitely been longer than a month. I wish I could say it was just a short spell, like a bad week or something, but it’s been going on for quite a while now.\n\nIf I think back on it, the voice and that feeling like people are watching us or checking on us… it’s been about eleven months. Not every single minute of every day, but it keeps coming back, and it’s been part of my life for most of this past year. It’s hard to admit that out loud, because when you say “eleven months” it sounds so much bigger than when you’re just trying to get through each day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any brain or nervous system illness?\n\nPatient: No… not that I know of. I mean, nobody has ever sat me down and told me I have some brain illness or nerve disease or anything like that.\n\nI’ve had regular aches and tiredness like people get, and I’ve been to doctors for ordinary things over the years, but as far as something with my brain or nervous system… I don’t have a diagnosis like that. If there was something in my chart, nobody explained it to me that way.\n\nDoctor: Since these problems began, have you been less able to do your work or take care of things at home than you were before?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I don’t like saying that, because I’ve always been someone who worked hard, especially with cleaning. I knew my routes, I knew what clients liked, and I used to be the person they trusted to keep things moving.\n\nBut since all this started, the agency took away my supervisory routes. They didn’t just say it out of nowhere—there were complaints, I guess, from clients and coworkers saying they couldn’t rely on my work the way they used to. That hurt a lot. I tried to explain that I was still trying, but it’s like things were slipping, and people noticed before I wanted to admit it.\n\nAnd at home, too… my husband has had to step in more. My pastor too, actually. Not just for emotional support, but with real things—making sure the caregiving and household responsibilities are handled safely, because I haven’t been keeping up like before. I used to manage so much of that myself, and now it feels like other people are having to watch over the things I should be handling. It’s embarrassing, but yes, I’m less able than I was.\n\nDoctor: During this past year, have you had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… I’ve had times where I’ve felt really worn down and upset, and some days I just don’t feel like myself, but if you’re asking whether there was a clear two-week stretch where I was depressed most of the day nearly every day… I don’t know that I can say that for sure.\n\nI don’t want to say yes if I’m not remembering it right. Sometimes everything kind of blends together when you’re just trying to keep going day by day. There have been bad days, definitely, but I’m not sure I noticed it in that kind of steady, every-day-for-two-weeks way.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a time when the voice and the feeling of being watched lasted for at least two weeks when you were not feeling unusually energized or depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there was. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Schizoaffective Disorder (Bipolar Type). You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because people keep telling me I’m imagining things, or that I’m “reading into it,” but I know what I’ve seen. It’s like they’re checking up on me, seeing if I’m taking proper care of my husband, like they don’t trust me to do it right.\n\nAnd it’s not just a feeling, you know? I’ve done this work a long time. I’m not stupid. I’ll hear a woman’s voice by the kitchen window, very clear, like she’s standing right there outside or just close enough that I should be able to see her.\n\nShe’ll say things like I need to check the locks, or that I should move my husband’s pills. And I know how that sounds, but it doesn’t feel like a thought in my own head. It sounds like someone speaking to me, plain as anything. I’ll look, and no one is there, but I still heard it. Other people will say I’m doing too much, but I feel like I’m just trying to stay on top of things.\n\nDoctor: When you hear that woman’s voice by the kitchen window, does it happen only during those times when you feel unusually energized?\n\nPatient: No… no, it isn’t only when I’m wound up like that. My family probably would’ve said I was calmer, or at least not constantly trying to get everything done at once. But even during that time, I still heard that woman’s voice by the kitchen window. And I still had that feeling—well, more than a feeling to me—that the agency people were watching us, checking on the house and whether I was handling things right.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t just happen when I have all that energy. It can happen even when I’m slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice and the sense that people are watching you been going on for longer than a month?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s definitely been longer than a month. I wish I could say it was just a short spell, like a bad week or something, but it’s been going on for quite a while now.\n\nIf I think back on it, the voice and that feeling like people are watching us or checking on us… it’s been about eleven months. Not every single minute of every day, but it keeps coming back, and it’s been part of my life for most of this past year. It’s hard to admit that out loud, because when you say “eleven months” it sounds so much bigger than when you’re just trying to get through each day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any brain or nervous system illness?\n\nPatient: No… not that I know of. I mean, nobody has ever sat me down and told me I have some brain illness or nerve disease or anything like that.\n\nI’ve had regular aches and tiredness like people get, and I’ve been to doctors for ordinary things over the years, but as far as something with my brain or nervous system… I don’t have a diagnosis like that. If there was something in my chart, nobody explained it to me that way.\n\nDoctor: Since these problems began, have you been less able to do your work or take care of things at home than you were before?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I don’t like saying that, because I’ve always been someone who worked hard, especially with cleaning. I knew my routes, I knew what clients liked, and I used to be the person they trusted to keep things moving.\n\nBut since all this started, the agency took away my supervisory routes. They didn’t just say it out of nowhere—there were complaints, I guess, from clients and coworkers saying they couldn’t rely on my work the way they used to. That hurt a lot. I tried to explain that I was still trying, but it’s like things were slipping, and people noticed before I wanted to admit it.\n\nAnd at home, too… my husband has had to step in more. My pastor too, actually. Not just for emotional support, but with real things—making sure the caregiving and household responsibilities are handled safely, because I haven’t been keeping up like before. I used to manage so much of that myself, and now it feels like other people are having to watch over the things I should be handling. It’s embarrassing, but yes, I’m less able than I was.\n\nDoctor: During this past year, have you had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… I’ve had times where I’ve felt really worn down and upset, and some days I just don’t feel like myself, but if you’re asking whether there was a clear two-week stretch where I was depressed most of the day nearly every day… I don’t know that I can say that for sure.\n\nI don’t want to say yes if I’m not remembering it right. Sometimes everything kind of blends together when you’re just trying to keep going day by day. During those times, I could sleep maybe two or three hours, and then I’d wake up like I’d had a full night. I’ll be making lists, then making another list because the first one wasn’t organized right, then I’m calling about appointments or trying to set things up, and before you know it I’ve taken on church errands too—like someone needs something dropped off, or there’s a call to make, or something to organize, and I’ll say, “I can do it,” even if my family is telling me I need to sit down and slow myself.\n\nBut at the time it doesn’t feel wrong to me. It feels like there’s finally enough energy to get everything handled, like if I don’t do it right then and there, it’ll all fall apart or get missed. Other people will say I’m doing too much, but I feel like I’m just trying to stay on top of things.\n\nDoctor: When you hear that woman’s voice by the kitchen window, does it happen only during those times when you feel unusually energized?\n\nPatient: No… no, it isn’t only when I’m wound up like that. I know why you’re asking, because I do have those stretches where I’m up early and doing too much, but there was a quieter time too—several weeks, I’d say—where I wasn’t rushing around the same way.\n\nI was sleeping more then, and I wasn’t taking on so much or moving so fast. My family probably would’ve said I was calmer, or at least not constantly trying to get everything done at once. But even during that time, I still heard that woman’s voice by the kitchen window. And I still had that feeling—well, more than a feeling to me—that the agency people were watching us, checking on the house and whether I was handling things right.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t just happen when I have all that energy. It can happen even when I’m slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice and the sense that people are watching you been going on for longer than a month?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s definitely been longer than a month. I wish I could say it was just a short spell, like a bad week or something, but it’s been going on for quite a while now.\n\nIf I think back on it, the voice and that feeling like people are watching us or checking on us… it’s been about eleven months. Not every single minute of every day, but it keeps coming back, and it’s been part of my life for most of this past year. It’s hard to admit that out loud, because when you say “eleven months” it sounds so much bigger than when you’re just trying to get through each day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any brain or nervous system illness?\n\nPatient: No… not that I know of. I mean, nobody has ever sat me down and told me I have some brain illness or nerve disease or anything like that.\n\nI’ve had regular aches and tiredness like people get, and I’ve been to doctors for ordinary things over the years, but as far as something with my brain or nervous system… I don’t have a diagnosis like that. If there was something in my chart, nobody explained it to me that way.\n\nDoctor: Since these problems began, have you been less able to do your work or take care of things at home than you were before?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I don’t like saying that, because I’ve always been someone who worked hard, especially with cleaning. I knew my routes, I knew what clients liked, and I used to be the person they trusted to keep things moving.\n\nBut since all this started, the agency took away my supervisory routes. They didn’t just say it out of nowhere—there were complaints, I guess, from clients and coworkers saying they couldn’t rely on my work the way they used to. That hurt a lot. I tried to explain that I was still trying, but it’s like things were slipping, and people noticed before I wanted to admit it.\n\nAnd at home, too… my husband has had to step in more. My pastor too, actually. Not just for emotional support, but with real things—making sure the caregiving and household responsibilities are handled safely, because I haven’t been keeping up like before. I used to manage so much of that myself, and now it feels like other people are having to watch over the things I should be handling. It’s embarrassing, but yes, I’m less able than I was.\n\nDoctor: During this past year, have you had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… I’ve had times where I’ve felt really worn down and upset, and some days I just don’t feel like myself, but if you’re asking whether there was a clear two-week stretch where I was depressed most of the day nearly every day… I don’t know that I can say that for sure.\n\nI don’t want to say yes if I’m not remembering it right. Sometimes everything kind of blends together when you’re just trying to keep going day by day. There have been bad days, definitely, but I’m not sure I noticed it in that kind of steady, every-day-for-two-weeks way.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a time when the voice and the feeling of being watched lasted for at least two weeks when you were not feeling unusually energized or depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there was. I’m trying to place it right, because I don’t want to make it sound more exact than it was, but there was a quieter spell for several weeks where I wasn’t all keyed up like before. I was sleeping more then, and I wasn’t rushing around so much or acting like I had to get everything done right that second.\n\nBut even during that calmer time, I still heard the voice by the kitchen window. And I still had that feeling that the agency was watching us. It didn’t go away just because I wasn’t as energized. I also don’t think I was deeply depressed then, not like lying there crying all day or anything like that. It was more like things had slowed down, but the watching feeling and the voice were still there in the background… sometimes not even background, honestly. It still felt real to me.\n\nDoctor: During those high-energy periods, did you need much less sleep than usual and still feel rested?\n\nPatient: Yes… that definitely happened. During those times, I could sleep maybe two or three hours, and then I’d wake up like I’d had a full night. Not groggy, not dragging myself around like I normally would if I slept that little.\n\nI’d get up and feel ready to do things right away—like scrub the floors, sort through papers, start cleaning things that didn’t even need to be cleaned at that hour. It wasn’t like when you can’t sleep and you feel miserable the next day. I felt awake, like my body didn’t need the rest. That’s part of what made it hard for me to see it as a problem at first, because I thought, well, at least I’m getting things done. But yes, I needed a lot less sleep and still felt rested.\n\nDoctor: During those high-energy times, did you find yourself talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure about that one. I mean, I know I was more wound up inside during those times, and I probably had more to say just because my mind felt busy, but I don’t know if I was talking much more than usual in a way other people would have noticed.\n\nSometimes I’m talkative anyway if I’m upset or if I feel like I need to explain myself, and other times I keep things in. So it’s hard for me to separate what was really different from just… me trying to get my thoughts out. I don’t remember anyone specifically saying, “You’re talking too much,” or anything like that. Maybe I talked faster or went on longer than I realized, but I can’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: During those high-energy times, did you feel especially powerful, important, or as if you had special abilities that other people didn’t?\n\nPatient: Yes… I did, though I don’t know if I would have called it “powerful” at the time. It felt more like I had to be the one to handle things, because nobody else was really seeing it clearly enough.\n\nI remember feeling like I was the only one with enough sense and strength to keep my husband safe, and to keep the house safe, and even some of the people from church. Like I had a responsibility on me that other people didn’t understand. It wasn’t just, “Oh, I’m better than everyone.” It was more like… I believed I had something in me, some kind of ability to notice what was coming and stand up to it when other people couldn’t.\n\nAnd I know that sounds like a lot when I say it out loud now, but at the time it felt very serious. I felt important in that way, like if I didn’t stay on top of things, something bad could happen and it would be because I failed to protect everyone.\n\nDoctor: At any time during this past year, did you have a period when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve had times where I felt really low, and I can get worn down, but “most of the day nearly every day for two weeks” is hard for me to pin down.\n\nI don’t keep track of it like that, you know? Some days feel heavy, and then other days I can still get up and do what I need to do, even if I don’t feel great. So I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure it was that steady or that long. It may have happened, but I honestly can’t clearly say, “Yes, it was every day like that for two weeks.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes, I think so. That part I can say more clearly. When I get like that, I don’t just sit around feeling energized — I start doing things, a lot of things, and usually too early.

I’ll be up before daylight, before anybody really needs to be moving, and I’ll already be cleaning something or cooking, like starting food when it’s not even time yet, wiping counters, sorting things, making piles, deciding the whole house needs to be straightened right then. And then I’ll start making lists — not just one list, but new lists because I think of something else, appointments I need to call about, things that need to be checked on, errands, what needs to be done for the house.

And I’d take on extra things from church too, like errands or helping with something, even when people would say, “You need to slow down,” or “That can wait.” But in my mind it couldn’t wait. It felt like if I had the energy and I could see what needed doing, then I should just do it. So yes, I was starting more things and putting more on myself than usual.

It feels like there’s finally enough energy to get everything handled, like if I don’t do it right then and there, it’ll all fall apart or get missed. Other people will say I’m doing too much, but I feel like I’m just trying to stay on top of things.\n\nDoctor: When you hear that woman’s voice by the kitchen window, does it happen only during those times when you feel unusually energized?\n\nPatient: No… no, it isn’t only when I’m wound up like that. I know why you’re asking, because I do have those stretches where I’m up early and doing too much, but there was a quieter time too—several weeks, I’d say—where I wasn’t rushing around the same way.\n\nI was sleeping more then, and I wasn’t taking on so much or moving so fast. My family probably would’ve said I was calmer, or at least not constantly trying to get everything done at once. But even during that time, I still heard that woman’s voice by the kitchen window. And I still had that feeling—well, more than a feeling to me—that the agency people were watching us, checking on the house and whether I was handling things right.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t just happen when I have all that energy. It can happen even when I’m slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice and the sense that people are watching you been going on for longer than a month?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s definitely been longer than a month. I wish I could say it was just a short spell, like a bad week or something, but it’s been going on for quite a while now.\n\nIf I think back on it, the voice and that feeling like people are watching us or checking on us… it’s been about eleven months. Not every single minute of every day, but it keeps coming back, and it’s been part of my life for most of this past year. It’s hard to admit that out loud, because when you say “eleven months” it sounds so much bigger than when you’re just trying to get through each day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any brain or nervous system illness?\n\nPatient: No… not that I know of. I mean, nobody has ever sat me down and told me I have some brain illness or nerve disease or anything like that.\n\nI’ve had regular aches and tiredness like people get, and I’ve been to doctors for ordinary things over the years, but as far as something with my brain or nervous system… I don’t have a diagnosis like that. If there was something in my chart, nobody explained it to me that way.\n\nDoctor: Since these problems began, have you been less able to do your work or take care of things at home than you were before?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I don’t like saying that, because I’ve always been someone who worked hard, especially with cleaning. I knew my routes, I knew what clients liked, and I used to be the person they trusted to keep things moving.\n\nBut since all this started, the agency took away my supervisory routes. They didn’t just say it out of nowhere—there were complaints, I guess, from clients and coworkers saying they couldn’t rely on my work the way they used to. That hurt a lot. I tried to explain that I was still trying, but it’s like things were slipping, and people noticed before I wanted to admit it.\n\nAnd at home, too… my husband has had to step in more. My pastor too, actually. Not just for emotional support, but with real things—making sure the caregiving and household responsibilities are handled safely, because I haven’t been keeping up like before. I used to manage so much of that myself, and now it feels like other people are having to watch over the things I should be handling. It’s embarrassing, but yes, I’m less able than I was.\n\nDoctor: During this past year, have you had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… I’ve had times where I’ve felt really worn down and upset, and some days I just don’t feel like myself, but if you’re asking whether there was a clear two-week stretch where I was depressed most of the day nearly every day… I don’t know that I can say that for sure.\n\nI don’t want to say yes if I’m not remembering it right. Sometimes everything kind of blends together when you’re just trying to keep going day by day. There have been bad days, definitely, but I’m not sure I noticed it in that kind of steady, every-day-for-two-weeks way.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a time when the voice and the feeling of being watched lasted for at least two weeks when you were not feeling unusually energized or depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there was. I’m trying to place it right, because I don’t want to make it sound more exact than it was, but there was a quieter spell for several weeks where I wasn’t all keyed up like before. I was sleeping more then, and I wasn’t rushing around so much or acting like I had to get everything done right that second.\n\nBut even during that calmer time, I still heard the voice by the kitchen window. And I still had that feeling that the agency was watching us. It didn’t go away just because I wasn’t as energized. I also don’t think I was deeply depressed then, not like lying there crying all day or anything like that. It was more like things had slowed down, but the watching feeling and the voice were still there in the background… sometimes not even background, honestly. It still felt real to me.\n\nDoctor: During those high-energy periods, did you need much less sleep than usual and still feel rested?\n\nPatient: Yes… that definitely happened. During those times, I could sleep maybe two or three hours, and then I’d wake up like I’d had a full night. Not groggy, not dragging myself around like I normally would if I slept that little.\n\nI’d get up and feel ready to do things right away—like scrub the floors, sort through papers, start cleaning things that didn’t even need to be cleaned at that hour. It wasn’t like when you can’t sleep and you feel miserable the next day. I felt awake, like my body didn’t need the rest. That’s part of what made it hard for me to see it as a problem at first, because I thought, well, at least I’m getting things done. But yes, I needed a lot less sleep and still felt rested.\n\nDoctor: During those high-energy times, did you find yourself talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure about that one. I mean, I know I was more wound up inside during those times, and I probably had more to say just because my mind felt busy, but I don’t know if I was talking much more than usual in a way other people would have noticed.\n\nSometimes I’m talkative anyway if I’m upset or if I feel like I need to explain myself, and other times I keep things in. So it’s hard for me to separate what was really different from just… me trying to get my thoughts out. I don’t remember anyone specifically saying, “You’re talking too much,” or anything like that. Maybe I talked faster or went on longer than I realized, but I can’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: During those high-energy times, did you feel especially powerful, important, or as if you had special abilities that other people didn’t?\n\nPatient: Yes… I did, though I don’t know if I would have called it “powerful” at the time. It felt more like I had to be the one to handle things, because nobody else was really seeing it clearly enough.\n\nI remember feeling like I was the only one with enough sense and strength to keep my husband safe, and to keep the house safe, and even some of the people from church. Like I had a responsibility on me that other people didn’t understand. It wasn’t just, “Oh, I’m better than everyone.” It was more like… I believed I had something in me, some kind of ability to notice what was coming and stand up to it when other people couldn’t.\n\nAnd I know that sounds like a lot when I say it out loud now, but at the time it felt very serious. I felt important in that way, like if I didn’t stay on top of things, something bad could happen and it would be because I failed to protect everyone.\n\nDoctor: At any time during this past year, did you have a period when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve had times where I felt really low, and I can get worn down, but “most of the day nearly every day for two weeks” is hard for me to pin down.\n\nI don’t keep track of it like that, you know? Some days feel heavy, and then other days I can still get up and do what I need to do, even if I don’t feel great. So I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure it was that steady or that long. It may have happened, but I honestly can’t clearly say, “Yes, it was every day like that for two weeks.”\n\nDoctor: During those high-energy times, did you find yourself starting a lot more projects or activities than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. That part I can say more clearly. When I get like that, I don’t just sit around feeling energized — I start doing things, a lot of things, and usually too early.\n\nI’ll be up before daylight, before anybody really needs to be moving, and I’ll already be cleaning something or cooking, like starting food when it’s not even time yet, wiping counters, sorting things, making piles, deciding the whole house needs to be straightened right then. And then I’ll start making lists — not just one list, but new lists because I think of something else, appointments I need to call about, things that need to be checked on, errands, what needs to be done for the house.\n\nAnd I’d take on extra things from church too, like errands or helping with something, even when people would say, “You need to slow down,” or “That can wait.” But in my mind it couldn’t wait. It felt like if I had the energy and I could see what needed doing, then I should just do it. So yes, I was starting more things and putting more on myself than usual.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It wasn’t just, “Oh, I’m better than everyone.” It was more like… I believed I had something in me, some kind of ability to notice what was coming and stand up to it when other people couldn’t.\n\nAnd I know that sounds like a lot when I say it out loud now, but at the time it felt very serious. I felt important in that way, like if I didn’t stay on top of things, something bad could happen and it would be because I failed to protect everyone.\n\nDoctor: At any time during this past year, did you have a period when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve had times where I felt really low, and I can get worn down, but “most of the day nearly every day for two weeks” is hard for me to pin down.\n\nI don’t keep track of it like that, you know? Some days feel heavy, and then other days I can still get up and do what I need to do, even if I don’t feel great. So I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure it was that steady or that long. It may have happened, but I honestly can’t clearly say, “Yes, it was every day like that for two weeks.”\n\nDoctor: During those high-energy times, did you find yourself starting a lot more projects or activities than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. That part I can say more clearly. When I get like that, I don’t just sit around feeling energized — I start doing things, a lot of things, and usually too early.\n\nI’ll be up before daylight, before anybody really needs to be moving, and I’ll already be cleaning something or cooking, like starting food when it’s not even time yet, wiping counters, sorting things, making piles, deciding the whole house needs to be straightened right then. And then I’ll start making lists — not just one list, but new lists because I think of something else, appointments I need to call about, things that need to be checked on, errands, what needs to be done for the house.\n\nAnd I’d take on extra things from church too, like errands or helping with something, even when people would say, “You need to slow down,” or “That can wait.” But in my mind it couldn’t wait. It felt like if I had the energy and I could see what needed doing, then I should just do it. So yes, I was starting more things and putting more on myself than usual.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid any of those high-energy periods last at least a week?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "patient_turn_index: 15\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."

Yes, I think some of them did. It wasn’t just like one busy day and then I was back to normal the next morning.

I’ve done this work a long time. I’m not stupid. But when I try to explain it, people look at me like I’m making it up, and that’s why I came in, because I need somebody to actually listen instead of just brushing me off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I do hear a voice sometimes, but it’s not like how people make it sound. It’s not constant, and I’m not just sitting there making it up. It usually happens when the house is quiet, like when everything has settled down and I’m near the kitchen. I’ll hear a woman’s voice by the kitchen window, very clear, like she’s standing right there outside or just close enough that I should be able to see her.\n\nShe’ll say things like I need to check the locks, or that I should move my husband’s pills. And I know how that sounds, but it doesn’t feel like a thought in my own head. It sounds like someone speaking to me, plain as anything. I’ll look, and no one is there, but I still heard it. So yes, I guess the answer is yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a time when you felt unusually high or energized for days at a time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have times like that. It’s not like I’m trying to be difficult or anything, but I’ll get up before it’s even daylight, before most people are thinking about moving, and I’m already cleaning, getting things straightened, starting food, checking what needs to be done.\n\nAnd then one thing turns into another. I’ll be making lists, then making another list because the first one wasn’t organized right, then I’m calling about appointments or trying to set things up, and before you know it I’ve taken on church errands too—like someone needs something dropped off, or there’s a call to make, or something to organize, and I’ll say, “I can do it,” even if my family is telling me I need to sit down and slow myself.\n\nBut at the time it doesn’t feel wrong to me. It feels like there’s finally enough energy to get everything handled, like if I don’t do it right then and there, it’ll all fall apart or get missed. Other people will say I’m doing too much, but I feel like I’m just trying to stay on top of things.\n\nDoctor: When you hear that woman’s voice by the kitchen window, does it happen only during those times when you feel unusually energized?\n\nPatient: No… no, it isn’t only when I’m wound up like that. I know why you’re asking, because I do have those stretches where I’m up early and doing too much, but there was a quieter time too—several weeks, I’d say—where I wasn’t rushing around the same way.\n\nI was sleeping more then, and I wasn’t taking on so much or moving so fast. My family probably would’ve said I was calmer, or at least not constantly trying to get everything done at once. But even during that time, I still heard that woman’s voice by the kitchen window. And I still had that feeling—well, more than a feeling to me—that the agency people were watching us, checking on the house and whether I was handling things right.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t just happen when I have all that energy. It can happen even when I’m slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice and the sense that people are watching you been going on for longer than a month?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s definitely been longer than a month. I wish I could say it was just a short spell, like a bad week or something, but it’s been going on for quite a while now.\n\nIf I think back on it, the voice and that feeling like people are watching us or checking on us… it’s been about eleven months. Not every single minute of every day, but it keeps coming back, and it’s been part of my life for most of this past year. It’s hard to admit that out loud, because when you say “eleven months” it sounds so much bigger than when you’re just trying to get through each day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any brain or nervous system illness?\n\nPatient: No… not that I know of. I mean, nobody has ever sat me down and told me I have some brain illness or nerve disease or anything like that.\n\nI’ve had regular aches and tiredness like people get, and I’ve been to doctors for ordinary things over the years, but as far as something with my brain or nervous system… I don’t have a diagnosis like that. If there was something in my chart, nobody explained it to me that way.\n\nDoctor: Since these problems began, have you been less able to do your work or take care of things at home than you were before?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I don’t like saying that, because I’ve always been someone who worked hard, especially with cleaning. I knew my routes, I knew what clients liked, and I used to be the person they trusted to keep things moving.\n\nBut since all this started, the agency took away my supervisory routes. They didn’t just say it out of nowhere—there were complaints, I guess, from clients and coworkers saying they couldn’t rely on my work the way they used to. That hurt a lot. I tried to explain that I was still trying, but it’s like things were slipping, and people noticed before I wanted to admit it.\n\nAnd at home, too… my husband has had to step in more. My pastor too, actually. Not just for emotional support, but with real things—making sure the caregiving and household responsibilities are handled safely, because I haven’t been keeping up like before. I used to manage so much of that myself, and now it feels like other people are having to watch over the things I should be handling. It’s embarrassing, but yes, I’m less able than I was.\n\nDoctor: During this past year, have you had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… I’ve had times where I’ve felt really worn down and upset, and some days I just don’t feel like myself, but if you’re asking whether there was a clear two-week stretch where I was depressed most of the day nearly every day… I don’t know that I can say that for sure.\n\nI don’t want to say yes if I’m not remembering it right. Sometimes everything kind of blends together when you’re just trying to keep going day by day. There have been bad days, definitely, but I’m not sure I noticed it in that kind of steady, every-day-for-two-weeks way.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a time when the voice and the feeling of being watched lasted for at least two weeks when you were not feeling unusually energized or depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there was. I was sleeping more then, and I wasn’t rushing around so much or acting like I had to get everything done right that second.\n\nBut even during that calmer time, I still heard the voice by the kitchen window. I also don’t think I was deeply depressed then, not like lying there crying all day or anything like that. It was more like things had slowed down, but the watching feeling and the voice were still there in the background… sometimes not even background, honestly. It still felt real to me.\n\nDoctor: During those high-energy periods, did you need much less sleep than usual and still feel rested?\n\nPatient: Yes… that definitely happened. During those times, I could sleep maybe two or three hours, and then I’d wake up like I’d had a full night. Not groggy, not dragging myself around like I normally would if I slept that little.\n\nI’d get up and feel ready to do things right away—like scrub the floors, sort through papers, start cleaning things that didn’t even need to be cleaned at that hour. It wasn’t like when you can’t sleep and you feel miserable the next day. I felt awake, like my body didn’t need the rest. That’s part of what made it hard for me to see it as a problem at first, because I thought, well, at least I’m getting things done. I mean, I know I was more wound up inside during those times, and I probably had more to say just because my mind felt busy, but I don’t know if I was talking much more than usual in a way other people would have noticed.\n\nSometimes I’m talkative anyway if I’m upset or if I feel like I need to explain myself, and other times I keep things in. So it’s hard for me to separate what was really different from just… me trying to get my thoughts out. I don’t remember anyone specifically saying, “You’re talking too much,” or anything like that. Maybe I talked faster or went on longer than I realized, but I can’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: During those high-energy times, did you feel especially powerful, important, or as if you had special abilities that other people didn’t?\n\nPatient: Yes… I did, though I don’t know if I would have called it “powerful” at the time.
